    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll eight generators are base (pretrained) models with $\\leq$8.2B parameters.\nInstruction-tuned variants show substantially degraded depth signal (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), and Gemma-2-2B exhibits near-zero gen-1 F1 (binary F1\\,=\\,0.440; gen-1 nearly undetectable) due to tokenizer and attention differences (Appendix~\\ref{app:9gen-scale}), indicating that architecture-specific generalization failures can occur; frontier models (70B+) remain untested.\n\nThe evaluation assumes a fixed decoding regime (temperature 0.7, nucleus $p{=}0.9$) with English Wikipedia seeds and an English-centric reference model (Pythia-410M); cross-temperature transfer degrades at mismatched settings (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-temp}), and non-English applicability is untested.\n\nDepth resolution is bounded at three classes (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+); finer-grained 5-class classification yields F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50 with deeper depths mutually indistinguishable (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}).\n",

    "newString": "These observations collectively suggest that recursive generation produces rapidly diminishing inter-iteration differences, consistent with the practical resolution limit we observe.",
    "originalFile": "\\vspace{-1mm}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\vspace{-1mm}\n\n%% Mechanism — why DDR exists\n\\looseness=-1 We hypothesize that DDR reflects a structural property of nucleus sampling: top-$p$ truncation preserves the conditional distribution's functional form while recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical, compressing expected NLL monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}); this hypothesis is testable but unproven.\nTwo testable predictions---increasing randomness weakens location drift, semantic constraints attenuate it---are both supported ($\\tau{=}1.0$: $\\rho{=}{-}0.002$; elaboration: F1\\,=\\,0.730; Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity},\\ref{app:prompt-type}), while shape stability holds across decoding strategies ($\\tau{=}1.0$, greedy, top-$k$; Appendices~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability},\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).\n\n%% Implications — what DDR enables\n\\looseness=-1 Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for base-model corpus auditing that binary human-vs-machine classifiers cannot access.\nThe classifier supports batch contamination estimation (${\\pm}3$--$4$\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}); depth-stratified curation has been proposed as a prophylactic against model collapse \\cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though no downstream improvement was observed (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\n\n%% Scope \\& Connections — where DDR works and fails\n\\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-supplement}); DDR complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).\nThese observations collectively suggest that recursive autoregressive generation produces rapidly diminishing inter-iteration differences, consistent with the practical resolution limit we observe.\n\\looseness=-1 Instruction tuning compresses DDR asymmetrically: location features lose monotonic depth correlation (6/7 show $\\rho$ reversal) while shape stationarity is partly preserved; the XGBoost classifier degrades by 3.7--19.9\\,pp, but QLoRA recovers to F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), suggesting depth signal persists in higher-dimensional representations.\nThree depth classes represent the current practical resolution limit under available approaches, both handcrafted and end-to-end (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}); cross-domain signal persists (F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\\,pp at 5\\%, F1\\,=\\,0.614 at 10\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

The vspace changes helped pull more Discussion text up, but the Conclusion still wraps to 4 lines and the last line still spills. The `\looseness=-1` can't compress it to 3 lines because the text is too long for 3 lines.

I need a more drastic approach. Options:
1. Cut even more text from the conclusion
2. Or try to save space elsewhere in the Discussion


3. Try a much shorter conclusion that captures the key findings without the extra detail

Let me try condensing it further: "Recursive generation produces decomposable depth responses enabling cross-generator classification (F1=0.694--0.762) across five families; extending DDR beyond base models remains open." This removes some of the intermediate phrasing and gets straight to the point, though I'm still worried about the line break at "across five."

Restructuring might help too—maybe moving the appendix reference inline or adjusting how the sentence flows to prevent that awkward break. ```text
Page 28 has massive whitespace too — just about 9 lines of text at the top, rest blank. This is the cross-temperature transfer section ending.

Page 29 has the DDR trends figure (Figure 8) which fills the page well.

Let me also check pages 35-40 for whitespace.

The main whitespace issues are on:
- Page 23: instruct section ends with 5 lines
- Page 28: cross-temp transfer ends with 9 lines

These are caused by `\FloatBarrier` forcing figures to be placed before the section ends, and the remaining text being too short to fill a page.


I can fix this by removing the problematic `\FloatBarrier` commands that are creating these gaps. Looking at main.tex, the `\FloatBarrier` after the instruct appendix on line 105-106 is causing the page 23 whitespace, and the one after the cross-temp section around line 131 is likely responsible for page 28. Removing these should let content flow naturally across section boundaries. causes page 28 whitespace

Let me remove these specific FloatBarriers and see if it helps. Actually, I should be more careful — removing FloatBarrier can cause figures to float to wrong sections.

A better approach: remove the FloatBarrier but use `[H]` for the figure placements to pin them in place. But the figures in instruct already use `[ht]` and `[H]`, so...

Actually, the instruct figure (`fig:instruct-ddr`) uses `[ht]` float. The problem is that when LaTeX has too many floats, it pushes them to a float page. With `\FloatBarrier`, the float page gets created before the next section starts, creating the gap.

Let me try changing the figure to use `[H]` (from the `float` package) which forces it to be placed exactly where it is in the source. This should eliminate the float page.

Looking at the instruct figure in appendix_instruct.tex, it's currently set to `[ht]`. I could change it to `[H]` to force inline placement, but the figure is quite tall with two stacked panels, so it might not fit well on the current page. Instead, I should focus on what the user actually wants — minimize whitespace and align content upward. For page 23, I could move the figure earlier in the source so it sits on a denser page, or remove the FloatBarrier from main.tex for that section. The same strategy applies to page 28.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XaRJeWZQ69n4MxVbHjZfSA`</summary>

```text
1	Figure~\ref{fig:cross-temp} shows cross-temperature transfer results for the 6-generator LOGO pipeline.
2	We evaluate four train$\rightarrow$test temperature conditions using Qwen3-1.7B data regenerated at $t{=}1.0$ (remaining five generators retain $t{=}0.7$).\footnote{The $t{=}1.0$ data uses top-$p{=}0.95$ (vs.\ $p{=}0.9$ for other configurations); this difference may contribute to the observed degradation beyond the temperature effect alone.}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[H]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_cross_temp.pdf}
7	\caption{Cross-temperature transfer evaluation (6-gen LOGO). (a) Overall F1 by train$\rightarrow$test temperature condition. Matched conditions (0.7$\rightarrow$0.7, 1.0$\rightarrow$1.0) outperform cross-temperature transfer, with 0.7$\rightarrow$1.0 showing the largest degradation ($-6.6$\,pp in 3-class F1). (b) Per-generator 3-class F1 heatmap: Qwen3-1.7B shows dramatic degradation under cross-temperature conditions (0.418) while other generators remain stable. The temperature mismatch disproportionately affects the generator whose data was regenerated.}
8	\label{fig:cross-temp}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	Key observations from the cross-temperature analysis:
12	
13	\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
14	\item \textbf{Matched-temperature robustness}: Both $t{=}0.7$ and $t{=}1.0$ matched conditions yield comparable performance (3-class F1: 0.789 vs.\ 0.726), with the gap attributable to weaker depth signal at high temperature rather than classifier degradation.
15	
16	\item \textbf{Asymmetric transfer}: Training at $t{=}1.0$ and testing at $t{=}0.7$ (F1\,=\,0.791) transfers well, but the reverse (0.7$\rightarrow$1.0, F1\,=\,0.723) degrades. This asymmetry reflects the information structure: features learned from high-variance (high-temperature) data generalize to low-variance settings, but not vice versa.
17	
18	\item \textbf{Generator-specific vulnerability}: Qwen3-1.7B, the only generator with temperature-mismatched test data, drops to F1\,=\,0.418 under cross-temperature conditions while all other generators maintain F1 $>$ 0.76. This confirms that depth features are calibrated to the specific decoding regime.
19	
20	\item \textbf{Binary transfer for the mismatched generator}: For Qwen3-1.7B (the only generator with actual temperature mismatch), binary F1 drops from 0.948 to 0.713 under 0.7$\rightarrow$1.0 transfer but recovers in the reverse direction (1.0$\rightarrow$0.7: 0.948), consistent with the asymmetric pattern in Item 2. The aggregate binary range (0.900--0.958) is inflated by five generators whose data is identical across conditions.
21	\end{enumerate}
22	
```

Let me think about how the ACL template actually handles figure placement... Looking at the LaTeX more carefully, the figure is constrained to `\columnwidth` and positioned with `[ht]`, which means it should occupy just one column in the two-column layout. The right column would be empty or contain text, but the rendered output makes it appear full-width because the figure panels are stacked vertically and take up significant vertical space on the page. TeX places single-column floats on their own page when they don't fit in a column, which explains why Figure 7 appears centered on page 24 and creates that awkward whitespace. The real solution is to split Figures 5, 6, and 7 into separate (a) and (b) figures so each can float independently—this lets (a) sit at the bottom of one page and (b) at the top of the next, which should resolve the page 24 and 38 spacing issues.

Let me check the current state of pages 17-19 to see exactly how these figures are laid out now.

I need to split Figures 5, 6, and 7 into separate `figure[H]` blocks to prevent text wrapping issues, and investigate what's causing the whitespace on page 38. I'll start by examining the current LaTeX files to understand their structure.

56	These results indicate that instruction tuning compresses the feature-space structure underlying DDR rather than simply reversing feature directions (Figure~\ref{fig:instruct-ddr}).
57	
58	\begin{figure}[ht]
59	\centering
60	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth, trim=0 0 205pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}\\[-2pt]
61	{\small (a) Base models (silhouette\,=\,0.660)}\\[6pt]
62	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth, trim=205pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}\\[-2pt]
63	{\small (b) Instruction-tuned models (silhouette\,=\,0.372)}
64	\caption{DDR partition under base vs.\ instruction-tuned models. Bars show pooled $|d|$; markers show individual generators. Instruction tuning compresses all features toward zero, collapsing the partition. Entropy max shifts from near-stationary to intermediate; formerly drifting features (hapax, TTR) lose most of their effect size.}
65	\label{fig:instruct-ddr}
66	\end{figure}
67	
68	\paragraph{Reference model sensitivity.}
69	One hypothesis for the instruct degradation is reference-model mismatch: the base Pythia-410M reference may produce systematically biased NLL estimates on instruction-tuned outputs, masking depth-dependent variation.
70	To test this, we replace the reference with Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct and recompute all features on the two instruct generators.
```

1	Table~\ref{tab:confusion} reports the aggregated confusion matrix from the original six-generator subset (Pythia-2.8B, Pythia-1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B) under leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) evaluation, collapsed to three ordinal classes: gen-0 (human), gen-1 (first-generation), and gen-2+ (depth $\geq$ 2).
2	The later-added Llama-3.2-3B and Mistral-7B-v0.3 are evaluated in the full 8-generator LOGO results (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
3	
4	\begin{table}[ht]
5	\centering
6	\small
7	\caption{Aggregated 3-class confusion matrix from 6-generator LOGO evaluation ($N=27{,}529$). Each cell shows count and row-normalized percentage. Gen-0 is near-perfectly identified. Gen-1 errors are asymmetric: misclassification as gen-2+ (18.8\%) is 3$\times$ more frequent than as gen-0 (6.2\%). Gen-1 text already carries detectable recursive distortion.}
8	\label{tab:confusion}
9	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
10	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
11	\toprule
12	& \textbf{Pred gen-0} & \textbf{Pred gen-1} & \textbf{Pred gen-2+} \\
13	\midrule
14	\textbf{True gen-0} & 5{,}937 (99.4\%) & 35 (0.6\%) & 0 (0.0\%) \\
15	\textbf{True gen-1} & 368 (6.2\%) & 4{,}437 (75.0\%) & 1{,}113 (18.8\%) \\
16	\textbf{True gen-2+} & 464 (3.0\%) & 3{,}646 (23.3\%) & 11{,}529 (73.7\%) \\
17	\bottomrule
18	\end{tabular}}
19	\end{table}
20	
21	Figure~\ref{fig:confusion-heatmap} provides a heatmap visualization of the confusion matrix.
22	The row-normalized view highlights near-perfect gen-0 classification (99.4\% recall), gen-1 confusion primarily with gen-2+ (18.8\%) rather than gen-0 (6.2\%), and gen-2+ misclassification predominantly into gen-1 (23.3\%), consistent with DDR predictions.

    "originalFile": "To verify that the depth signal resides in the features rather than the classifier,\nwe repeat the 6-generator LOGO evaluation using Logistic Regression and Random Forest\nin place of XGBoost (Table~\\ref{tab:alt-classifier}).\nAll other settings (the same 7 features, 3-class labels, and class-balanced downsampling) are\nkept identical. For Logistic Regression we apply standard scaling to the features.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{LOGO macro-F1 across three classifiers. Each column shows the held-out generator F1. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}\n\\label{tab:alt-classifier}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lc cccccc}\n\\toprule\nClassifier & Avg F1 & OLMo-2 & Py-1B & Py-2.8B & Qw-0.6B & Qw-1.7B & Qw-8B \\\\\n\\midrule\nXGBoost          & 0.789 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.824 & 0.806 & 0.683 \\\\\nRandom Forest    & 0.790 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.825 & 0.807 & 0.684 \\\\\nLogistic Regr.   & 0.725 & 0.719 & 0.781 & 0.740 & 0.745 & 0.743 & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth tree-based classifiers achieve LOGO F1 $\\approx 0.79$,\nand even a linear model reaches $0.725$, well above the $0.33$ chance baseline for\nthree classes.\nThe per-generator ranking is also consistent across classifiers\n(Pythia-1B easiest, Qwen3-8B hardest),\nconfirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,\nnot in classifier capacity.\nOrdinal-specific methods (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) achieve comparable F1 with overlapping CIs, which confirms that 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses to improve over standard multiclass classification.\n\n\\subsection*{Sampling Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:sampling-sensitivity}\n\nTo assess robustness to decoding temperature, we regenerate continuation chains at $t \\in \\{0.5, 0.9\\}$ with fixed nucleus $p{=}0.9$ (matching the default) for four generators spanning three model families: Pythia-2.8B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, and Qwen3-0.6B (Table~\\ref{tab:sampling-ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Depth classification under varying temperature (fixed top-$p$\\,=\\,0.9). Binary: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+. LOGO evaluation; 95\\% bootstrap CIs.$^{\\ast}$$^{\\dagger}$}\n\\label{tab:sampling-ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc c}\n\\toprule\nModel & Temp & LOGO Binary F1 & 95\\% CI & $\\rho$(ent.\\ mean) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-2.8B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.5  & \\textbf{0.814} & [.798, .828] & $-0.58$ \\\\\nPythia-2.8B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.7  & 0.740 & [.725, .755] & $-0.60$ \\\\\nPythia-2.8B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.9  & 0.480 & [.465, .498] & $-0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B & 0.5  & \\textbf{0.783} & [.768, .798] & $-0.72$ \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B & 0.7  & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & $-0.60$ \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B & 0.9  & 0.453 & [.437, .469] & $-0.26$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-1.7B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.5  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.7  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.9  & 0.566 & --- & $-0.37$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-0.6B & 0.5  & 0.711 & [.694, .729] & $-0.67$ \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B & 0.7  & 0.691 & [.673, .709] & $-0.60$ \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B & 0.9  & 0.598 & [.582, .615] & $-0.29$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\begin{minipage}{\\columnwidth}\n\\scriptsize $^{\\ast}$OLMo temperature experiments use OLMo-2-0425-1B (April 2025 release); the original OLMo-2-1B-hf weights are no longer publicly available.\\par\\vspace{1pt}\n$^{\\dagger}$Pythia-2.8B and Qwen3-1.7B use 6-generator LOGO (pre-expansion); OLMo-2-1B and Qwen3-0.6B use 8-generator LOGO. Within-model comparisons are unaffected.\n\\end{minipage}\n\n\\begin{sloppypar}\nTemperature sensitivity is asymmetric and model-dependent. High-temperature degradation ($t{=}0.9$) is universal: all four generators lose $9.3$--$26.0$\\,pp in binary F1, with entropy mean's $|\\rho|$ weakening to $0.26$--$0.37$ (vs.\\ $0.58$--$0.72$ at $t{=}0.5$). Low-temperature improvement ($t{=}0.5$) is model-dependent: Pythia-2.8B and OLMo-2-1B gain $+7.4$ and $+8.8$\\,pp respectively, while both Qwen models show negligible change ($0$--$2.0$\\,pp). This split tracks model family rather than scale: Qwen3-0.6B (smallest) and Qwen3-1.7B both plateau, while OLMo-2-1B (similar scale to Qwen3-1.7B) benefits substantially. One possible explanation is that Qwen models produce more peaked output distributions at $t{=}0.7$, leaving less room for low-temperature compression to sharpen the depth signal.\n\\end{sloppypar}\n\nThe method operates reliably within $t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$ across all tested models. Degradation onset at $t{=}0.9$ defines the practical upper bound, consistent with the hypothesis that elevated temperature injects sufficient randomness to flatten the distributional drift that DDR exploits (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:per-gen} visualizes per-generator LOGO performance.\nPythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.\nThe scale effect (larger models $\\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 347pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=347pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap}\n\\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. Dashed lines in (a): pool average. Heatmap in (b) shows the performance spread across model families.}\n\\label{fig:per-gen}\n\\end{figure}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}\n\\label{tab:instruct}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Binary F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{\\rho}$\\textbf{(ent.\\ mean)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \\textbf{Rev.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nInstruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\\Delta| \\leq 3.2$\\,pp).\nThe asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.\nEntropy-mean $\\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\\ 3 of 7 for Llama.\nThis pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}\nTo test whether the depth signal persists under instruction tuning with reversed polarity, we retrain the XGBoost classifier on instruction-tuned feature data (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-retrain}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classification recovery under instruction-tuned retraining (binary-only: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, gen-0 excluded from training and evaluation). Base$\\rightarrow$Inst: base-trained classifier tested on instruct data. Note: Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reports higher instruct binary F1 (e.g., OLMo 0.813) because it uses 3-class training with binary evaluation, where gen-0 in training helps the classifier learn the feature space.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-retrain}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Base$\\rightarrow$Inst} & \\textbf{Retrained} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.321 & 0.590 & $+$0.269 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.500 & 0.660 & $+$0.160 \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B-Inst  & 0.532 & 0.600 & $+$0.068 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nRetraining recovers substantial within-model performance ($+$6.8--26.9\\,pp), but the retrained F1 (0.590--0.660) remains below base-model levels (0.69--0.72).\nThree-generator instruct LOGO yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.487, indicating weak cross-generator transfer under instruction tuning.\n\n\\paragraph{DDR structure under instruction tuning.}\n$k{=}2$ clustering in $(|\\rho|, d)$ space on pooled instruct features yields silhouette\\,=\\,0.372 (vs.\\ 0.660 for base models), indicating that the clean drifting/non-drifting partition is substantially compressed.\nThe compression is model-dependent: OLMo-Instruct shows near-zero $|\\rho|$ for TTR and hapax (0.006, 0.025), while Qwen instruct models retain moderate correlations ($|\\rho|{=}0.48$--$0.53$).\nCross-condition transfer is near chance in both directions (base$\\rightarrow$instruct binary F1\\,=\\,0.490; instruct$\\rightarrow$base F1\\,=\\,0.454), with the asymmetry ($-$0.036) suggesting that instruct-trained features generalize even less to base distributions than the reverse.\nThese results indicate that instruction tuning compresses the feature-space structure underlying DDR rather than simply reversing feature directions (Figure~\\ref{fig:instruct-ddr}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 205pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) Base models (silhouette\\,=\\,0.660)}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=205pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) Instruction-tuned models (silhouette\\,=\\,0.372)}\n\\caption{DDR partition under base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models. Bars show pooled $|d|$; markers show individual generators. Instruction tuning compresses all features toward zero, collapsing the partition. Entropy max shifts from near-stationary to intermediate; formerly drifting features (hapax, TTR) lose most of their effect size.}\n\\label{fig:instruct-ddr}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model sensitivity.}\nOne hypothesis for the instruct degradation is reference-model mismatch: the base Pythia-410M reference may produce systematically biased NLL estimates on instruction-tuned outputs, masking depth-dependent variation.\nTo test this, we replace the reference with Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct and recompute all features on the two instruct generators.\nThe hypothesis is partially rejected: Qwen3-1.7B-Instruct shows mixed changes (entropy max $d$: $0.51 \\to 0.08$, becoming near-stationary; entropy std $d$: $1.07 \\to 1.51$, increasing), while OLMo-Instruct loses nearly all depth signal (entropy mean $d$: $1.33 \\to 0.02$; tail decay $d$: $0.60 \\to 0.06$).\nThe model-dependent heterogeneity rules out reference mismatch as a universal explanation for instruct DDR degradation, supporting the interpretation that alignment fundamentally reshapes the generation dynamics rather than introducing a correctable measurement artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end instruct classification.}\nTo test whether depth signal persists at the token level despite summary-statistic degradation, we apply the QLoRA end-to-end classifier (Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) to instruction-tuned data using within-model 5-fold stratified CV (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{End-to-end QLoRA vs.\\ XGBoost on instruction-tuned data (within-model 5-fold CV, Qwen3-0.6B backbone). Base QLoRA rows shown for reference only; instruct sets ($N{\\approx}4.4$--$5.0$K) are approximately twice the base sets ($N{\\approx}2.3$K), so cross-condition comparison is confounded by training set size.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-qlora}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\n\\textbf{Model} & XGB & QLoRA & $\\boldsymbol{\\Delta}$ & \\textbf{$N$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.575 & 0.888\\,$\\pm$\\,0.007 & $+$31.3\\,pp & 4{,}429 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.732 & 0.907\\,$\\pm$\\,0.008 & $+$17.5\\,pp & 4{,}963 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B (base) &       & 0.859\\,$\\pm$\\,0.004 &             & 2{,}379 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B (base)&       & 0.904\\,$\\pm$\\,0.009 &             & 2{,}291 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe XGBoost-to-QLoRA gap on the same instruct data is substantial ($+$17.5 to $+$31.3\\,pp), indicating that depth signal persists at the token level under instruction tuning but is inaccessible to the seven summary statistics; the degradation reported in Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reflects a feature-level limitation rather than signal absence.\nNote that instruct samples are approximately twice the base count used here (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}), so the instruct-vs-base QLoRA comparison is confounded by training set size; the meaningful comparison is QLoRA-vs-XGBoost on the same instruct data.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV, not cross-generator LOGO, limiting generalization claims.\n",

1	\subsection{9-Generator Scale Evaluation}\label{app:9gen-scale}
2	
3	This analysis extends the 8-generator LOGO evaluation to nine generators by adding Gemma-2-2B, a model from a previously unrepresented architecture family (Gemma).
4	All results use class-balanced LOGO; the 8-gen averages here (0.770/0.701) differ slightly from Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} (0.762/0.694), which uses an unfiltered protocol.
5	Table~\ref{tab:9gen-logo} reports per-generator LOGO performance under this expanded set.
6	
7	\begin{table}[ht]
8	\centering
9	\caption{9-generator LOGO evaluation. Each row holds out the listed generator at test time. 3-class: gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+; binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+. 95\% bootstrap CIs in brackets.}
10	\label{tab:9gen-logo}
11	\small
12	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
13	\begin{tabular}{llr cc cc}
14	\toprule
15	\textbf{Generator} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
16	\midrule
17	Pythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & 0.857 & [0.848, 0.866] & 0.785 & --- \\
18	Qwen3-0.6B      & Qwen    & 0.6B & 0.819 & [0.807, 0.830] & 0.743 & --- \\
19	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & 0.812 & [0.801, 0.822] & 0.728 & --- \\
20	Qwen3-1.7B      & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.811 & [0.800, 0.823] & 0.746 & --- \\
21	OLMo-2-1B       & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.773 & [0.762, 0.784] & 0.692 & --- \\
22	Llama-3.2-3B    & Llama   & 3.2B & 0.759 & [0.748, 0.770] & 0.680 & --- \\
23	Qwen3-8B        & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.696 & [0.683, 0.709] & 0.647 & [0.631, 0.664] \\
24	Gemma-2-2B      & Gemma   & 2.6B & 0.628 & [0.622, 0.634] & 0.440 & [0.432, 0.449] \\
25	Mistral-7B      & Mistral & 7.0B & 0.605 & [0.593, 0.617] & 0.534 & --- \\
26	\midrule
27	\textbf{Average (9-gen)} & & & \textbf{0.751} & & \textbf{0.666} & \\
28	\textbf{Average (8-gen)} & & & \textbf{0.770} & & \textbf{0.701} & \\
29	\bottomrule
30	\end{tabular}}
31	\end{table}
32	
33	\paragraph{Overall comparison.}
34	Adding Gemma-2-2B reduces the mean 3-class LOGO F1 from 0.770 (8-gen) to 0.751 (9-gen), a drop of $\Delta = -0.019$.
35	The per-generator F1 values for the original eight generators shift by at most $[-0.011, +0.002]$, indicating that the inclusion of Gemma data in training does not destabilize existing generalization patterns.
36	
37	\paragraph{Gemma-2-2B anomaly.}
38	Gemma-2-2B is the weakest held-out generator: 3-class F1\,=\,0.628, binary F1\,=\,0.440.
39	Per-class breakdown reveals a striking asymmetry: gen-0 F1\,=\,0.991 and gen-2+ F1\,=\,0.856, but gen-1 F1\,=\,0.036; the classifier almost entirely fails to identify first-generation Gemma continuations.
40	This is not a data-quality artifact: the Gemma-2-2B partition contains 4{,}957 instances with a balanced depth distribution ($\sim$990 per level).
41	A plausible explanation is that Gemma-2's first-generation continuations occupy a region of feature space highly similar to human-written text (gen-0), causing systematic misclassification of gen-1 as gen-0 or gen-2+.
42	This constitutes an architecture-specific generalization limitation rather than a systemic failure of the depth profiling approach.
43	Several architectural differences may explain this behavior: (a)~Gemma-2's distinct attention mechanism and SentencePiece tokenizer with a larger vocabulary may produce first-generation continuations whose feature-space signature closely resembles human text, causing systematic gen-0/gen-1 confusion; (b)~this anomaly does not affect DDR's validity for the remaining eight generators, where per-generator F1 shifts by at most 0.011 upon Gemma inclusion.
44	
45	\paragraph{Scale effect.}
46	A clear parameter-count gradient emerges across the nine generators: three of four sub-1.7B generators achieve F1\,$>$\,0.81 (OLMo-2-1B at 0.773 is the exception), mid-range models (2--3B) cluster at 0.63--0.81, and large models (7B+) fall to 0.60--0.70.
47	Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters) achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.696 under the 9-gen split, compared to 0.701 in the class-balanced 8-gen evaluation; this small difference reflects the changed training composition rather than a property of the Qwen3-8B generator itself.
48	
49	\paragraph{14B-parameter evidence.}
50	Preliminary evidence from a 14.7B generator (Qwen3-14B-Base) confirms that the DDR partition persists at larger scale: Cohen's $d$ for drifting features remains in the large-effect range ($d{=}0.78$--$1.12$), though Spearman $|\rho|$ attenuates by 21--35\%, with entropy mean falling just below the drifting threshold ($|\rho|{=}0.39 < 0.40$, $d{=}0.78$).
51	This scale-dependent signal compression is consistent with larger models producing more self-typical outputs that narrow inter-depth contrasts; the partition structure is preserved even as absolute effect sizes compress.
52

Each row holds out the listed generator at test time. 3-class: gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+; binary: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+. 95\\% bootstrap CIs in brackets.}\n\\label{tab:9gen-logo}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llr cc cc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Generator} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{3-class F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Binary F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & 0.857 & [0.848, 0.866] & 0.785 & --- \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B      & Qwen    & 0.6B & 0.819 & [0.807, 0.830] & 0.743 & --- \\\\\nPythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & 0.812 & [0.801, 0.822] & 0.728 & --- \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B      & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.811 & [0.800, 0.823] & 0.746 & --- \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B       & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.773 & [0.762, 0.784] & 0.692 & --- \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B    & Llama   & 3.2B & 0.759 & [0.748, 0.770] & 0.680 & --- \\\\\nQwen3-8B        & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.696 & [0.683, 0.709] & 0.647 & [0.631, 0.664] \\\\\nGemma-2-2B      & Gemma   & 2.6B & 0.628 & [0.622, 0.634] & 0.440 & [0.432, 0.449] \\\\\nMistral-7B      & Mistral & 7.0B & 0.605 & [0.593, 0.617] & 0.534 & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Average (9-gen)} & & & \\textbf{0.751} & & \\textbf{0.666} & \\\\\n\\textbf{Average (8-gen)} & & & \\textbf{0.770} & & \\textbf{0.701} & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Overall comparison.}\nAdding Gemma-2-2B reduces the mean 3-class LOGO F1 from 0.770 (8-gen) to 0.751 (9-gen), a drop of $\\Delta = -0.019$.\nThe per-generator F1 values for the original eight generators shift by at most $[-0.011, +0.002]$, indicating that the inclusion of Gemma data in training does not destabilize existing generalization patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Gemma-2-2B anomaly.}\nGemma-2-2B is the weakest held-out generator: 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.628, binary F1\\,=\\,0.440.\nPer-class breakdown reveals a striking asymmetry: gen-0 F1\\,=\\,0.991 and gen-2+ F1\\,=\\,0.856, but gen-1 F1\\,=\\,0.036; the classifier almost entirely fails to identify first-generation Gemma continuations.\nThis is not a data-quality artifact: the Gemma-2-2B partition contains 4{,}957 instances with a balanced depth distribution ($\\sim$990 per level).\nA plausible explanation is that Gemma-2's first-generation continuations occupy a region of feature space highly similar to human-written text (gen-0), causing systematic misclassification of gen-1 as gen-0 or gen-2+.\nThis constitutes an architecture-specific generalization limitation rather than a systemic failure of the depth profiling approach.\nSeveral architectural differences may explain this behavior: (a)~Gemma-2's distinct attention mechanism and SentencePiece tokenizer with a larger vocabulary may produce first-generation continuations whose feature-space signature closely resembles human text, causing systematic gen-0/gen-1 confusion; (b)~this anomaly does not affect DDR's validity for the remaining eight generators, where per-generator F1 shifts by at most 0.011 upon Gemma inclusion.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale effect.}\nA clear parameter-count gradient emerges across the nine generators: three of four sub-1.7B generators achieve F1\\,$>$\\,0.81 (OLMo-2-1B at 0.773 is the exception), mid-range models (2--3B) cluster at 0.63--0.81, and large models (7B+) fall to 0.60--0.70.\nQwen3-8B (8.2B parameters) achieves 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.696 under the 9-gen split, compared to 0.701 in the class-balanced 8-gen evaluation; this small difference reflects the changed training composition rather than a property of the Qwen3-8B generator itself.\n\n\\paragraph{14B-parameter evidence.}\nPreliminary evidence from a 14.7B generator (Qwen3-14B-Base) confirms that the DDR partition persists at larger scale: Cohen's $d$ for drifting features remains in the large-effect range ($d{=}0.78$--$1.12$), though Spearman $|\\rho|$ attenuates by 21--35\\%, with entropy mean falling just below the drifting threshold ($|\\rho|{=}0.39 < 0.40$, $d{=}0.78$).\nThis scale-dependent signal compression is consistent with larger models producing more self-typical outputs that narrow inter-depth contrasts; the partition structure is preserved even as absolute effect sizes compress.\n",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 52
    }
